# MORNING PRAYER.

Another night has passed! I wake up, and once again I experience the pleasure of existing. It is toward you, infinite goodness <note>The author addresses the Divine as "infinite goodness," a phrasing common in late 18th and early 19th-century spiritual writing that emphasizes a benevolent, loving God.</note>, that I lift my first thought—you who gave me life, you who watch over me during my sleep!

O, how great your goodness must be! You take care of the least of your creatures. Yes, you are necessarily good; and this thought that you are good shall never leave my mind. How consoling it is for me! With what delight do I behold this world again, where everything reminds me of your benevolence and your paternal care!

I am your creature; I am but dust, yet your omnipotence <term>omnipotence: all-encompassing or infinite power</term> brings it to life. I feel within me a heart that opens to sensibility <note>In this period, "sensibility" (sensibilité) referred to a refined capacity for emotion, empathy, and moral awareness.</note>, a soul capable of trust...

<vocab>Morning prayer, infinite goodness, creature, omnipotence, sensibility</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a lyrical morning prayer centered on gratitude for existence and the protective care of a benevolent creator. It reflects a transitional spiritual style that blends traditional piety with Enlightenment-era "sensibility."</summary>
<keywords>morning prayer, gratitude, providence, divine benevolence, Enlightenment spirituality, sensibility</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical exploration of spiritual duty, transitioning from abstract definitions of God to a personal, moral commitment to humanitarianism.</meta>

To know God is to keep His commandments, and His commandments are nothing but love. What does that indefinable being, who created me, require of me? What are the commandments of the Master of the Universe? Love, pure love; this is the answer given by all of nature. Love God, love yourself, love your neighbor <note>original: "ton prochain" - a term often used in a biblical sense to mean any fellow human being.</note>. I had a father, and I loved him; I have a friend, and my heart leaps at our meeting; I have a benefactor, and my soul is moved when I think of him. And you, eternal God, you are my father, my friend, and my benefactor—could I possibly fail to love you? 

O fellow men, my equals, how dear you are to me! You are all my brothers, and when I embrace you, I embrace within you the Eternal <note>original: "l'Eternel" - a common French Enlightenment-era designation for God, emphasizing His timeless nature.</note>, our common father. Receive then this morning, beneficent Creator of all beings, the solemn vow I make to follow Your sacred example. I desire from this moment forward to succor the unfortunate, protect the oppressed, take in orphans, clothe those who are naked, heal those who are wounded, and bring back those who... <note>The sentence ends abruptly at the bottom of the page.</note>

<summary>The author reflects on the nature of divine command, concluding that true religion consists of universal love and humanitarian action. The page culminates in a solemn vow to dedicate his life to aiding the poor, the oppressed, and the suffering.</summary>

<keywords>universal love, humanitarianism, Enlightenment deism, brotherhood, divine commandments, charity, social duty</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the prayer of commitment from page 7, completing the sentence regarding those who have lost their way.</meta>

( 8 )

...have gone astray. I will be gentle toward my subordinates, full of love toward those whom you have entrusted to my care, and I will look upon every creature formed in your image as my brother, as a child who is dear to you.

The feelings that I express here, Lord, I feel them in the depths of my heart; see in me the sincerity of a child, you who penetrate into the deepest recesses <note>original: "replis" — literally "folds," a poetic way of describing the hidden or complex parts of the human psyche</note> of my soul.

Thus receive, in the first moments of this day, the first proofs of my love. I forgive, for your sake, all my enemies, and I promise you, Lord, to do good to whoever has done me harm, to bless the one who curses me, and to cherish the one who hates me. I also intend never to speak ill of my fellow man, to never interpret any of his actions in a negative light, nor to turn him into an object of ridicule, nor to mistreat him, even when he has offended me. I intend not to scandalize him by any bad example, to

<summary>This page continues a personal devotional prayer where the author pledges to practice radical empathy, forgiveness, and moral restraint. He vows to treat all people as brothers and to respond to hatred and offense with kindness and prayer.</summary>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page concludes the morning prayer of commitment and begins a new theological reflection on human nature and its place in the hierarchy of creation.</meta>

( 9 )

never be **unjust** toward him, to never wickedly lead him into error, but to act constantly toward him as you have commanded us. If you bless him, I shall not be envious <note>original: "en vieux" — likely an OCR error for "envieux" (envious)</note>; I will help him if he needs my help.

Am I unable to help him in fact? I will help him with my counsel; I will guide him. I will conduct myself thus, Lord, to obey your holy commandments, and out of love for you.

Everything reveals to us that we are but travelers here below, and he who knows and feels his calling does not stop uselessly on this journey; by following the path traced for him by <term>Providence</term> <note>the protective care and guardianship of God</note>, he hastens to make himself worthy of the next life, and he awaits the signal from the <term>Eternal One</term> <note>original: "l'Eternel"; a common 18th and 19th-century title for God</note> for his departure.

Associated with the animals through our bodies, we are associated with <term>immortal spirits</term> through the <term>soul</term> <note>original: "ame"</note>. Our body is held in the slavery of the senses; decay awaits our outward remains; but freedom and <term>immortality</term> are reserved for our spirit. <meta>This page completes the word "now" (maintenant) from the end of page 12 and continues the author's teleological argument, moving from sensory self-awareness to the recognition of a Creator and the shared social nature of existence.</meta>

now here, and who did not exist before. I am, I feel my existence. And who placed me here? Who gave this admirable structure to my body? Who endowed me with the precious faculty of enjoying everything that surrounds me? To which being am I indebted for these benefits? I owe them to him who created these magnificent globes <note>Likely referring to the planets and stars, reflecting the 18th-century view of the universe as a grand, ordered machine created by a "Great Architect."</note>, and whose paternal care extends to everything. But what are the pleasures of material nature compared to the feelings of the soul, compared to those tender emotions he has placed in my heart?

If I were isolated on the earth, the most pleasant joys of nature would soon become insipid to me; but his beneficent hand has given me thousands of creatures as companions; it has placed in my heart an exquisite sensitivity to feel, in concert, the pleasures that others feel. Everything surrounding me is alive; a thousand diverse insects crawl upon the smallest of these leaves, all of them live, all of them experience his goodness.

Here, a thousand birds gladden the grove with their song in joyful competition; there, the majestic lion roars with pleasure; here, a turtledove coos; there, I hear the linnet whistling; further away, the warbler hops and hums; all rejoice in their existence. I am in the midst of them all; I see, I feel, I share their pleasures; but I also feel an inner power, a secret movement, which reveals to me that I bear a resemblance to the one who created me.

An inner voice tells me: Enjoy the life given to you. A thousand sentiments then, a thousand movements previously unknown, rise in my heart; friendship and love reign there together. Here, the movements of nature bind me to beloved parents, sympathy to a faithful friend, marriage <note>The original French uses "l'hymen," a poetic term for marriage common in the 18th century, referencing the Greek god of marriage ceremonies.</note> and love to a tender wife, paternal affection to children; all these bestowed sentiments <note>The author uses "sentimens étrangers," which literally means "foreign sentiments." My inner sense <term>The "inner sense" (sens intime) was a popular philosophical concept in the 18th century, referring to an intuitive, God-given awareness of moral truth and self-existence.</term> dictates it to me; it is written in my heart in indelible characters.

The desire to see everyone around me happy is the most vivid and sweet of all desires. All is happiness for man; even misfortune in this world is a preparation for happiness in the next. If we are not happy here below through our own means, we become so by sharing in the happiness of our fellow beings. The purest of the soul’s sentiments, which you have awakened in us, is that of sharing the joy and misfortune of others. Yes, it is only by loving, by loving in the way that you command us, that I feel the full value of my existence.

# ELEVATION

### OF ONE'S SENTIMENTS TOWARD THE CREATOR. <note>An "Elevation" (Élévation) is a specific genre of meditative prayer, popularized by 17th-century theologians like Bossuet, intended to lift the soul toward spiritual contemplation.</note>

Great God! grant me, I beseech you, the innocence of wisdom; for I recognize that even the angels are only wise before you because their soul is innocent and pure. I recognize that I can attribute nothing of the good that is in me to myself; but that it is to you, my Lord and my God, that I owe everything.

Evil is my own doing; but good <note>The sentence ends abruptly here, suggesting a continuation on the following page regarding the source of virtue.</note>

<vocab>vine, ivy, Creator, innocence, wisdom, angels, soul</vocab>

<summary>The author uses metaphors of climbing plants to illustrate the human need for divine and mutual support, followed by a prayer acknowledging that all personal goodness stems from God.</summary>
<keywords>nature metaphors, interdependence, divine love, Elevation, humility, innocence, source of good</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page completes the sentence fragment from page 17: "Evil is my own doing; but good..." The author continues his "Elevation," a form of contemplative prayer, focusing on the source of virtue and the relationship between love for God and love for one's neighbor.</meta>

which is within me is yours, O my Creator and my Sustainer! <note>original: "conservateur." In a theological context, this refers to "Divine Conservation," the belief that God doesn't just create the world and leave, but actively sustains its existence at every moment.</note>

Inexhaustible light of truth, I beseech you, be my guide in the difficult paths of my pilgrimage; it is by you alone, and not by the false glimmer of my pride, my presumption, and my self-love <note>original: "amour propre." This term was frequently used by French moralists to describe a corrupted, vain form of self-interest as opposed to "amour de soi," which is natural self-preservation.</note>, that I wish to be led. I cry out to you, Lord, be my leader and my guide. I will love all that you teach me to be good; I will delight in all that is true, because you are goodness and truth itself; I know and I feel that to love the good, to will it, and to perform it, is to love God; to love the true, to will it, and to perform it, is to love one's neighbor. Permit me then to love you, and to love my neighbor as myself.

I am content with all that you have given me, Lord, because I know that I have received all that was beneficial to me, and because you have taught me that he who has received little also had need of little.

<vocab>Creator, Sustainer (Preserver), Light, truth, pilgrimage, pride, presumption, self-love, Lord, neighbor</vocab>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s prayerful "elevation," moving from the acceptance of one’s lot (established on page 17/18) to a meditation on the contrast between divine and human nature. The focus shifts from the author's internal state to the character of the Creator.</meta>

( 19 )

Limited creature <note>original: "créature bornée." This term reflects a common 18th-century philosophical and theological perspective, emphasizing the finite nature of human intellect and power compared to the infinite nature of the Divine.</note> that I am; I do not know what is beneficial to me; but you know it, Lord, you whose providence <term>providence: the belief in God's active, ongoing intervention and protective care over the universe and the lives of individuals</term> extends to everything. Whoever truly hopes in you, whoever clings to you, cannot perish.

Lord, I implore you, for you are my father; whom else but you can I implore? Who else loves me as much as you love me? Who else but you reaches out his arms to me in misfortune?

You are eternal, immutable love, always ready to listen to the prayers of your creatures, never deaf to the voice of your children. You do not resemble men, who so casually shut their doors to the unfortunate, who give today and take back tomorrow, who regret the morsel they throw away, or who rid themselves of you by means of their servants <note>The author here critiques the coldness of human charity, where the wealthy might feel a sense of loss ("regret") when giving or use servants as a buffer to avoid direct contact with the poor.</note>. No, you do not resemble them at all; the monarch before you is no more than the beggar; we are all your children.

<summary>The author contrasts the fickle, conditional nature of human charity with the steadfast and egalitarian love of God. He surrenders his own limited judgment to divine providence, asserting that all people—regardless of social rank—are equal in the eyes of the Creator.</summary>

Assuredly not; his love had no beginning; he loves from all eternity; he has loved for thousands, for millions of years. He is love itself, the source of all love; his desire to destine beings resembling himself for a happiness similar to his own existed with him from all eternity.

## FEAR OF GOD.

What is the fear of the Lord? That is the subject of my reflections today. I remember that, in my childhood, I feared wicked beings, beings who caused me harm; the fear of the Lord cannot be such a fear. God is love and goodness: can one tremble before love and goodness itself? From my childhood, how many times, during my solitary walks in the middle of the countryside, did I not admire the touching signs of his goodness! The simple wildflower reminded me of it, the blooming rose inspired confidence in my heart, and the scented violet breathed forth beneficence <term>beneficence: the quality of doing good or being kind; a spirit of active charity</term>.

<vocab>Fear of God, Lord, love, goodness, childhood, reflections, beneficence</vocab>

<summary>The author continues their prayer for divine likeness before pivoting to reflect on the "fear of God," arguing that it is not a terrifying dread of harm but rather something distinct from the fear of wicked beings. The meditation emphasizes that God’s nature is found in the beauty and goodness of the natural world, such as flowers and the countryside.</summary>
<keywords>fear of God, divine goodness, childhood memories, nature and divinity, beneficence, spiritual reflection</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the reflection on the "Fear of God" initiated on page 30. It further defines this "fear" not as terror, but as a filial anxiety of displeasing a loving creator, contrasting God's authority with that of earthly tyrants.</meta>

The anxiety of acting against the will of the one we love—that is fear in its purity; to fear what is contrary to divine love is pure and salutary <note>original: "salutaire" — beneficial or promoting health; in a spiritual context, leading to salvation</note> fear; it is the fear born of love.

It is in this way, and no other, that I wish to fear you, O my God! You do not at all resemble the great ones of the earth: they have slaves crawling at their feet, trembling as they receive their orders; but your relationship with mortals is that of a father with his children; love and trust form the chain that unites you with mankind. To offend you voluntarily is therefore a monstrous ingratitude.

May I, O my God, whenever I stray from the path of virtue, return to it soon! You forgive our offenses when repentance snatches us away from our fatal blindness. The penitent David <note>A reference to the biblical King David, who famously sought God’s mercy through sincere repentance after his sins (notably in Psalm 51).</note> deserved for you to forgive his crime; this thought reassures me. I hope in your divine goodness; you do not reject a heart that returns sincerely to you; you stretch out to us

# KNOWLEDGE
## OF ONE’S DESTINY.

I have often acted in a manner opposite to the designs of divine love; I must therefore seek to know my destiny.

Love God, love your neighbor as yourself; that is your destiny, that is your vocation <note>original: "vocation" — from the Latin 'vocare' (to call), referring to one’s spiritual calling or purpose in life</note>. To love your neighbor as yourself

This last precept is the height of evangelical perfection <note>original: "perfection évangélique" — the moral and spiritual completeness taught in the Gospels, particularly the Sermon on the Mount</note>; never has a purely philosophical morality been able to rise to this sublime, this heroic self-abnegation. Yes, you are obliged, not only to forgive your enemies, but to love them, and to help them when you can. The gentle sentiments that the Divinity has placed in your heart, for your preservation <note>original: "conversation" — likely an archaic usage or variant for "conservation," referring to the natural instincts for survival and well-being</note> and for your happiness, extend them also, through your actions, to the creatures who are like you: treat them as your equals; that is to say, whatever gives you pleasure, grant it also to your neighbor; whatever afflicts you, spare your neighbor from it. This is the law that God has written in your heart. I therefore carry the book of the law with me everywhere; I feel every day what is just and what is not.

We owe to Jesus Christ the sacrifice of all our passions: that of hatred is by no means excluded from it; one must love even their enemies.

How does love act? Love watches over the well-being of the beloved object; it is gentle, compassionate, and merciful; it forgives, it is not self-interested, it acts without selfishness, and solely for the sake of its love.

Thus, I must love God for God’s sake.

My neighbor for their own sake, and myself only as much as is necessary in order to reach the goal prescribed by the Divinity in creating me, and in order to preserve myself for the well-being of my fellow beings.

This is the measure of true love. Is it then so painful to lead a life that leads us to heaven? Less painful than I had thought. Practicing these virtues is an indispensable duty. Renouncing all wealth, mortifying the flesh <note>The author refers to "mortification," a traditional religious practice of self-denial or physical discomfort to strengthen spiritual discipline</note>, sleeping on ashes, and fasting are useless sacrifices if one does not also join to them the sacrifice of forgiving one’s greatest enemies.

If I consider human life, I see it under a double aspect: the spiritual or moral life, and the civil life. The spiritual or moral life must desire what is good; the civil life must carry it out.

<summary>The author establishes a hierarchy of love where God and neighbor take precedence over the self and argues that true virtue lies in forgiveness rather than outward ascetic practices. He introduces a distinction between the "spiritual life" of intentions and the "civil life" of actions.</summary>

<keywords>hierarchy of love, altruism, asceticism, forgiveness, spiritual life, civil life, moral duty, Christian ethics</keywords>

# ON THE TRUE AND THE GOOD.

If I look around me, Lord, and contemplate the beauty of nature, if I examine the wisdom of Your works, everything declares to me that truth and goodness <note>original: 'vérité' and 'bonté'</note> are the foundations upon which heaven and earth rest.

It is therefore necessary that I learn to know what is true and what is good. Let us examine this important subject.

You alone, Lord, are truth and goodness by <term>essence|In theological terms, 'essence' refers to the fundamental nature of God that does not change; God does not just 'possess' truth, He 'is' Truth.</term>, and there is nothing true or good except what You are. To know love is the good; and to practice love is the true. To love and to know is the good; to love and to practice, that is the true. Thus, truth and goodness must be united; for truth is the subject of Your knowledge, and the

<vocab>Lord, nature, wisdom, truth, goodness, love, the good</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a meditation on the nature of Truth and Goodness, defining them as the dual foundations of the universe and as divine attributes that must be both known and practiced through love.</summary>
<keywords>divine attributes, truth, goodness, nature, wisdom, essence of God, practice of love</keywords>

good is the subject of the will. And what would knowledge be without the will?

The intelligence and the wisdom of Your angels exist only through the union of the true with the good: without this agreement, there is nothing but error and falsehood.

You are therefore truth, O my God! and all that You are is the true by <term>essence|The fundamental nature of a being; the author suggests that God does not merely possess truth as a quality, but is Truth itself.</term>. Thus, when I seek the truth, it is You whom I must seek, and You whom I must resemble.

You have given me, Lord, the will and the intelligence: intelligence to know, and the will to desire <note>original: 'vouloir' - translated here as 'desire' or 'will' to reflect the active choice of the soul</note> that which I have come to know.

You, my God, are goodness by essence: everything that has existence is good, and everything that approaches the practice of the good is true. When I recognize Your goodness, and I transform this knowledge into will, then I make Your goodness visible, and my action is true.

# KNOWLEDGE OF THE ETERNAL.

### THE VOCATION OF MAN, THE DUTY OF MAN.

**Technical Vocabulary**
*   **Pure love:** Love directed toward God or the good without any expectation of reward.
*   **Selfishness:** The state of being concerned only with one's own interests.
*   **Love of myself:** Individual self-interest.
*   **Self-love:** Vanity or prideful ego (original: *amour-propre*).
*   **Public good:** The collective well-being of the community.
*   **Master of the universe:** A common Enlightenment-era reference to God as the rational architect of creation.

<summary>The author identifies selfishness and "amour-propre" (vanity) as the principal enemies of spiritual progress and pure love. He defines an egoist as someone who acts only for personal glory or ignores the welfare of their neighbor and the common good.</summary>

# PRAYERS OF THE MASS.

->*Upon entering the temple of the Lord.*<-

My God! As I enter your temple, grant that I may find stillness and look within myself <term>recueillement|A French spiritual term describing a state of inner silence and withdrawal from the world to focus entirely on the presence of the Divine.</term>, so that I may worship you in spirit and in truth. <note>This is a direct reference to Jesus’s words to the Samaritan woman in John 4:24: "God is spirit, and those who worship him must worship in spirit and truth."</note> Inspire me with pure thoughts regarding the sublimity of your being. <meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections from page 67. # E P I S T L E. <note>original: "E P I T R E." In this context, an epistle is a formal, poetic letter addressed to a person or deity to convey moral or spiritual truths.</note>

Eternal and uncreated Being! Wise creator of this vast universe! I recognize that your goodness has assigned this magnificent theater <note>This is a common 18th-century metaphor where the natural world is viewed as a grand stage designed by God for the benefit and instruction of humanity.</note> to men as a dwelling. Allow me to address my prayers to you, as my sovereign master, and to love you as the father of all beings. Yes, you are as good as you are great; everything tells me so, and above all, my heart. Oh! May all men adore you, each in their own way and following what their heart dictates to them that is most tender and most affectionate! You deign to speak to us, almighty creator, through the resounding voice of nature. Our entire service toward you consists of adoring you, thanking you, and crying out even to your throne that we are weak, miserable, limited <note>original: "bornées," referring to the finite nature of human understanding compared to the infinite nature of God.</note> creatures, and that we need your helping hand.

### THE LAST GOSPEL.
<note>In the traditional Roman Rite (Tridentine Mass), the "Last Gospel" refers to the reading of John 1:1–14 at the very end of the service, which speaks of the Word becoming flesh.</note>

> Teach me to know, Lord my God, that your doctrine alone leads men to true happiness; make me renounce all illusory happiness and grant me the grace to understand <note>original: "concevoir" – to grasp or perceive</note> that self-love and love of the world only lead me away from my great destiny. May all my actions be as pure as the morning dew, and may I transform my heart into a temple worthy of becoming your dwelling place! May your gospel be the rule of my conduct, and may Jesus Christ be the sacred model that I set before me until my final breath <note>original: "dernier soupir" – literally "last sigh"</note>.

# PRAYERS
## FOR CONFESSION.

---

Grant me the grace, O my God, to know and to sincerely confess my faults. I ask you most humbly for pardon; may my repentance disarm your anger. Make me abstain from sin in the future, and may my life be entirely dedicated to works of love and faith. Yes, Lord, it is to your service that I wish to devote myself entirely; I implore your assistance; make me avoid all occasions for committing faults <note>In Catholic theology, "occasions of sin" refers to external circumstances—such as certain people, places, or things—that entice or lead a person to commit a sin.</note>. May your love keep me in the paths of justice and virtue. Guide my steps, enlighten me; may the fear of offending you serve as a salutary guide <note>original: "salutaire" – meaning beneficial to one's spiritual health or salvation</note> for me.

<term>Prayers</term> (original: *Prières*) — Personal or communal communications with the divine.

<term>Pardon</term> (original: *pardon*) — Forgiveness granted for an offense or sin.
<term>Repentance</term> (original: *repentir*) — Sincere regret or remorse for one's past actions.
<term>Sin</term> (original: *péché*) — An immoral act considered to be a transgression against divine law.
<term>Faith</term> (original: *foi*)
<term>Lord</term> (original: *Seigneur*)
<term>Justice</term> (original: *justice*) — Righteousness and moral uprightness in one's conduct.
<term>Virtue</term> (original: *vertu*) — Behavior showing high moral standards.

### TO BE ENLIGHTENED IN THE EXAMINATION OF ONE'S CONSCIENCE.

> God, author of all light, you know the inner folds of my heart and all my sins are written before you. <note>The "folds of the heart" is a traditional poetic way of describing the secret or hidden parts of one's character and memory.</note>
>
> Enlighten me, so that I may also know them and that I may give myself a strict account of my past conduct.
>
> I wish to penetrate into the deepest part of my inner self, and omit none of my faults, so that I may deplore them, all without exception, with all the sorrow of which I am capable.
>
> Give me your grace as a guide in this judgment of myself, and may your Holy Spirit—whose assistance you promised us through the mouth of your son when we should ask for it—descend upon me and spread its clarity into my soul, so that I may know the number and the gravity <note>original: "grièveté" – referring to the seriousness or weight of a sin</note> of the offenses I have committed through thoughts, words, and actions, toward you, toward my neighbor, and toward myself.

<term>Holy Spirit</term> (original: *esprit saint*) — The third person of the Christian Trinity, often called upon for guidance, wisdom, and clarity of mind.
<term>Offenses</term> (original: *offenses*) — Specific instances of wrongdoing or sin.
<term>Thoughts, words, and actions</term> (original: *pensées, paroles et actions*) — The traditional threefold classification used in Catholic liturgy (the Confiteor) to ensure a comprehensive review of one's behavior.

# AN EASY AND REASONABLE METHOD FOR EXAMINING ONE'S CONSCIENCE.

How have I lived since [a certain] time? <note>original: "depuis tel temps" — This refers to the interval of time since the individual's last confession.</note> In what way did I begin and spend this day? Has it been among those days that are lost for eternity? Could I finish the course of my life with this very day? <note>This is a common "memento mori" reflection, asking the believer if they are spiritually prepared to die at this moment.</note>

How does my heart stand with God? Do I often think of his presence in all places, of his goodness, of his holy law? Do I speak of him willingly and with the proper respect? Do I pray to the Lord every day, and how? Do I thank him for the many blessings <note>original: "bienfaits" — refers to the favors, graces, or good things received from divine providence</note> I enjoy? What have I done for the love of him? Have I listened to and followed his inspirations?

How have I behaved toward my brothers? <note>In this religious context, "brothers" refers to one's neighbors or fellow human beings.</note> Have bitterness, hatred, vengeance, contempt, the satisfaction of seeing them unhappy, or suspicions inhabited my heart in turn? Is my heart now pure of all these passions? <note>In 18th and 19th-century devotional language, "passions" refers to intense emotional impulses—like anger or pride—that were thought to lead the soul away from reason and virtue.</note>

<term>Examination of conscience</term> (original: *examen de conscience*) — A systematic reflection on one's past actions and thoughts to identify sins.
<term>God</term> (original: *Dieu*)
<term>Holy law</term> (original: *loi sainte*) — The divine commandments and moral obligations.
<term>Prayer</term> (original: *prière*)
<term>Blessings</term> (original: *bienfaits*) — Acts of divine grace or kindness.
<term>Inspirations</term> (original: *inspirations*) — Spiritual promptings or "inner voices" believed to come from God.
<term>Brothers</term> (original: *frères*) — Fellow men; neighbors.
<term>Passions</term> (original: *passions*) — Strong, often negative, emotional states that disturb the soul’s peace.

### RESOLUTION TO REFORM
<note>In devotional literature, this is known as a "Resolution of Amendment"—a firm commitment made during prayer to change one's behavior and avoid future sins.</note>

God of love, how many times have I not promised you to reform myself, and how many times have I not broken my vow and abandoned the pure ways of your love!

Self-love <note>In this spiritual context, "self-love" (*amour-propre*) refers to pride or ego that places one's own desires above God's will.</note> and love of the world have always led me away from you, eternal creator! Let me recognize that only your love can create my happiness. Father of humanity! I do not wish to fill up the measure of my iniquities <term>iniquities: a formal term for sins or acts of gross injustice.</term>, nor abuse your goodness any longer. I resolve to obey you in the future and to be faithful to your commandments. But, Lord, what are my resolutions without the help of your grace <term>grace: the divine assistance given to humans for their regeneration or sanctification.</term>?

Sincerity, it is true, resides in my heart, but I recognize how weak I am and that I can do nothing without your assistance.

<vocab>pardon, reconciliation, resolution to reform, God of love, vow, iniquities, commandments, grace</vocab>

<summary>The author transitions from seeking forgiveness to making a formal resolution to improve their life, acknowledging that human will is too weak to resist worldly temptations without the help of divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>resolution of amendment, self-love, divine grace, repentance, commandments, spiritual weakness</keywords>

### BEFORE COMMUNION.

### ACT OF ADORATION.

> Lord, my existence and my life are nothing to you, but you are everything to me. You were God before I existed, and you will still be God long after my body no longer resembles a human form. # PRAYER
### FOR A FRIEND OR FOR A BENEFACTOR.

God of love! It is to you that I am indebted for the sweet feelings of love and friendship. It is you who, by placing them in my heart, have granted me the most fortunate of human joys. O creator of the happiness of mortals, pour out salvation and blessing upon the one whom my heart cherishes. Preserve him from the dangers that beset life and protect him, so that evil may never reach him. May the first rays of a beautiful sun make pleasure shine in his eyes, and may the last rays still illuminate the smile of pleasure upon his lips! Send him comforting dreams during his sleep, and spread joy throughout his heart.

# PRAYER

### FOR DECEASED PARENTS OR FRIENDS.

Lord, allow me the memory of these dear people, who are now beyond the grave. Death has closed their eyes; they are no longer here for me. # ACTS OF THANKSGIVING

### FOR IMMORTALITY.

O supreme Happiness! Inundate my soul with a torrent of flame; I am going to meditate on the Eternal <note>original: "l’Eternel"; a common French designation for God, emphasizing His timelessness</note>. You have been, you are, and you will be. What name shall I give you? My trembling lips fall silent.

How can I think of you, infinite Being? How can I conceive of you? My soul stops in wonder; my spirit cannot reach you. Father! O Father of mankind! It is thus that my lips may stammer your name, it is thus that my soul may think of you—

## PRAYER
### OF A FATHER FOR HIS CHILDREN.

The children I have, Lord, are a gift from your goodness. You have placed within me a feeling that makes them dear to my heart: the sentiment of **paternal love**.

Lord, what you have given me also belongs to you. I therefore return your gifts to you: deign to be their father; I am but a wretched mortal, what can I do for them without you?

The tree I have planted will wither where it stands if it is not moistened by the dew of heaven, and if it is not watered by the rains that rise from your clouds. # PRAYER

->IN ADVERSITY.<-

Lord, the clouds of misfortune are gathering over my head, and consuming sorrows are ready to descend <note>The original French "fondre sur moi" implies a sudden, bird-of-prey-like swooping down or pouncing.</note> upon me. But I do not murmur <note>To "murmur" in a religious context refers to complaining or grumbling against God’s will, a reference to the Israelites in the desert.</note> against you, Lord; you do not cease to be for me the God of love; you are the unshakable rock upon which I have built the edifice of my hopes. You are not unaware, Lord, of why all these events happen, and you take care of my fate. I know that you love me, I know that you love all mankind, and the certainty of this thought is all my strength. It will not be adversity that conquers me, but I myself shall conquer adversity; I will fight with courage the afflictions of this life, and I will not let them win the victory over my heart through my cowardice.

I know that you have made the things of this world subordinate to me, O my God, and that it is not I who am subordinate to them. What can happen to me, when you watch over me, God of strength and love!

# PRAYER

->DURING WAR.<-

I lift my heart to you, Lord, father of all men; grant us, I beseech you, indulgence <note>Here, "indulgence" refers to divine forbearance or mercy toward human failings.</note> and forgiveness. This term refers to deep-seated injustices or sins that violate divine law.</note>.

# SENTIMENTS
### INTERNAL
### OF A SOUL TOWARDS GOD.
<note>original: "SENTIMENS INTERIEURS D'UNE AME ENVERS DIEU"</note>

O you! Eternal source of all good! You from whom all comes, by whom all is preserved, and to whom all must return for the fulfillment of the great designs of your love and your wisdom! You, in all things, the alpha and the omega, the first and the last, the beginning and the end! <note>The Alpha and Omega are the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, a traditional title for God signifying His eternity and role as the creator and final goal of the universe.</note> You, whose omnipotence and love brought everything into being, whose truth and wisdom preserve all, whose goodness and justice rule all! Principle of harmony and concord, love, truth, wisdom, goodness, order, and beauty of all things! I prostrate myself before you, and I acknowledge that I am nothing without you, and that I am everything through you.

# SACRIFICE
### OF THE WILL.

It is toward you alone that I wish to raise my heart, the primal source of all good, all truth, and all beauty.

It is you alone who will direct my reason, my will, and my activity; you shall be my sole <term>prime mover</term> <note>Original: "unique moteur." This term, rooted in Aristotelian and Thomistic philosophy, refers to God as the primary cause of all motion and action in the universe.</note>.

I will cast aside all that is not you; all that is of you, I will receive with open arms. It is you alone whom I wish to love, you alone whom I wish to seek in all things. I will love all people for the love of you; I will bear their faults for the love of you; for the love of you, I will forgive them.

I will seek you within passing things as the One who never passes away, and within beings subject to changes and variations, as the One who experiences neither variation nor change.

<vocab>Sacrifice of the will, Jesus Christ, Savior, reason, activity, love, forgiveness</vocab>

<keywords>Sacrifice of the Will, spiritual submission, prime mover, divine love, constancy, forgiveness, mysticism</keywords>

### ACT OF LOVE AND REPENTANCE.

My Lord and my God, I love you with all my heart, because you are the <term>supreme good</term> <note>The "supreme good" (summum bonum) refers to God as the ultimate and highest source of all value, happiness, and moral perfection.</note>, infinite, and most worthy of all love and all happiness. I also love my <term>neighbor</term> <note>The French "prochain" literally means "the one who is near," following the biblical commandment to love all fellow humans as oneself.</note> and even my enemies, whom I forgive for the love of you, because my neighbor is created in your image and because my divine master also loved his enemies even upon the cross. I wish to live and die in this love. O God! kindle within me the fire of your love.

It is by virtue of this pure love that I repent with a <term>contrite heart</term> <note>A "contrite heart" is one filled with sincere sorrow for sins, motivated by a love for God rather than a fear of punishment.</note> for all...

# PRESENCE
### OF GOD IN EVERY PLACE.

Savior of men! when you struggled against death; when, in this <term>garden of sorrows</term> <note>This refers to the Garden of Gethsemane, where, according to the Gospels, Jesus prayed in agony before his arrest and eventual crucifixion.</note>, you lifted your spirit to your father, and the blood and water streaming over your body soaked the earth beneath your knees,

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the meditation on the "Presence of God in Every Place," specifically picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's description of Jesus's prayer in the Garden of Gethsemane.</meta>

in this terrible moment, you taught us a truth, which will remain truth as long as men are men. You rise and you say to your sleeping disciples: "The spirit is <term>willing</term> <note>original: "docile." In this context, it refers to the spirit's readiness or compliance with God's will.</note> but the flesh is weak."

This condition common to all finite beings, this earthly weight—my soul feels it too when it seeks to rise toward you, <term>uncreated Being</term> <note>A theological term for God, signifying that he is the source of all existence and was not created by any other power.</note>, infinite Being.

I prostrate myself in the dust, O my father, to adore you and to beseech you. Listen to the prayers, hear the voice of a limited being, Lord. Fill my soul with fire, so that it may rise up to you.

You are present in all places; you surround me, O my father. Let my reflection pause here, and let it savor this delicious thought of your omnipresence!

What will the sight of the Eternal face to face be for a weak mortal, if the mere thought of your presence inspires such supernatural strength in me? What will your appearance be for me, eternal, infinite Being!

<summary>The author reflects on the human struggle between spiritual aspiration and physical weakness, drawing comfort and strength from the concept of God's omnipresence.</summary>
<keywords>Agony in the Garden, human nature, divine presence, omnipresence, prayer, transcendence</keywords>

# THOUGHTS
### ON THE REDEMPTION.

O God! how can my spirit conceive of this thought of your supreme love, the thought of our redemption? I vaguely discern the greatness of your work through the sacred veil that covers it, without my spirit being able to fathom <note>original: "approfondir." Literally to go deeper into; to fully comprehend or grasp the depths of a subject.</note> it. And how could it fathom you, this insect <note>A common metaphorical device in 18th and 19th-century spiritual writing used to contrast human insignificance with divine majesty.</note> that does not even know the ever-enduring miracle of the sun; that can determine neither its course nor its structure, though it feels the beneficent warmth of its rays every day? No, my soul, never will you fathom the divinity, the sublimity of this decree which made Jesus a victim of death; you will [not]

<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page (192), concluding the thought that while the intellect cannot grasp the mystery of the Redemption, the heart can feel its effects. know how to do anything but feel the divine effects of the love that dictated it.

O human wisdom! you who are but folly <note>original: "folie." This is likely a reference to the New Testament concept (1 Corinthians 1:25) that "the foolishness of God is wiser than human wisdom."</note> in the eyes of Eternal Wisdom, do not rob me of the consolation of thinking that the Son of God has erased my faults upon the tree of the cross.

With what despairing anxiety would I not be troubled now, if God had not been my redeemer! Let the mockery of the impious <note>The author is likely defending his faith against Enlightenment-era skeptics or "philosophes" who critiqued traditional religious doctrines as irrational.</note> not take from me the consolation that faith provides; I experience its divine efficacy, I adore and I believe: I find peace and rest in this holy belief; the rest that no mortal can give. I believe, and already I feel my heart rising, and striving to become like Him who is all love, and who loved us unto death; I believe, and this belief makes this earthly stay more pleasant for me, my heart is more human for it and my soul more strong.

<vocab>Eternal Wisdom, Son of God, cross, redeemer, faith, belief</vocab>

<summary>The author rejects purely intellectual critiques of Christianity, arguing that the peace and moral strength provided by faith in the Redemption are proof of its truth. He concludes that faith makes the heart more "human" and the soul stronger during its time on earth.</summary>

## MERCY.

O my God, God of mercy, my soul rises toward you. The Lord is your name; your name is Father of mankind. You speak, and worlds emerge from nothingness <note>original: "néant." In theological French, this refers to the absolute void before God’s creation.</note>; you speak, and worlds return to nothingness. A sea of bliss surrounds your throne. O, permit a drop of this sea to moisten my palate, so that I may taste of that happiness which you reserve for those who love you.

...rise beyond the limits prescribed for it. Direct my will according to these luminous laws of your wisdom, and grant that my heart and my spirit follow the movements of this will with ardor.

Human reason falls silent before you, Incomprehensible Being; but my heart reaches out unceasingly toward you. Draw it ever more toward the source of your light, until my soul, fulfilling its desires, rises from its material prison <term>material prison: a common philosophical and mystical metaphor suggesting the physical body constrains the immortal soul</term> into the homeland of pure spirits, and is reunited with you, Lord, who was, who is, and who will be for all eternity.

#### P R A Y E R.

**B**lessed Virgin! Mother of my God and in this capacity worthy of the deepest veneration from angels and men! I turn to you to pay you the hom- <note>original: "hom-"; likely "hommages" (homage or respects).</note>

age due to your holiness and to implore your powerful protection.

->*Hail Mary.*<- 
<note>original: "Je te salue, Marie." This is the opening of the Ave Maria.</note>

I acknowledge you and I honor you as the mother of my God and of my Savior. I acknowledge you and I honor you as my most powerful intercessor <note>intercessor: a figure who petitions God or Jesus on behalf of a believer</note> before the throne of your son, my God and my Savior, and I entrust myself to your protection and to your prayers.

->*Full of grace.*<-
<note>original: "Pleine de grace."</note>

God has filled you with His grace. He has preserved you without stain and pure from all sin, and He has endowed you with all the virtues and all the perfections that were fitting for the Mother of God. <note>This reflects the theological concept of Mary being uniquely chosen and sanctified for her role.</note>

->*The Lord is with you.*<-
<note>original: "Le Seigneur est avec toi."</note>

He has been with you without ceasing, and no sin has ever separated Him from you. He accompanied you throughout your life with His grace and His protection, and He is still and shall remain with you for all eternity, in all the fullness of His glory and His majesty.

*Blessed are you among all women.*
<note>original: "Tu es bénie entre toutes les femmes." This phrase comes from the greeting of Elizabeth, Mary's cousin, during the Visitation.</note>

God has blessed you, and from among all those of your sex He chose you to be the mother of Him in whom all generations must be blessed.

*And blessed is the fruit of your womb, Jesus.*
<note>original: "Et béni soit le fruit de ton ventre, Jésus." This completes the biblical portion of the prayer.</note>

You were blessed by Him, we bless you with Him, and we bless you because of Him. Blessed is the body that carried Him! Blessed is the breast that nourished Him! But more blessed is she who has always loved Him, who heard the word of God and kept it as the rule of her conduct! <note>The author is referencing the Gospel of Luke (11:27-28), suggesting that Mary's spiritual obedience is even more significant than her biological motherhood.</note>

->*Holy Mary, Mother of God.*<-
<note>original: "Sainte Marie, mère de Dieu." This begins the second, "petitionary" half of the prayer, which was formalized by the Church in the 16th century.</note>

You who conceived Jesus, who is God, and who brought Him into the world; you whose intercession <note>intercession: the act of praying to God on behalf of others</note> is all-powerful with your son; whose prayers can achieve everything with the Almighty, and whose kindness toward humanity equals your power in heaven, be favorable to us.

# PRAYER
### BY ELIZABETH ROWE.

O SOURCE of true love, allow my thirsting soul <note>original: "ame altérée." This imagery of a "thirsting soul" is a common mystical motif, suggesting a deep, spiritual longing for the divine.</note> to draw from the delightful torrents that flow from you! Let it plunge into the plenitude of heavenly joys, where its desires, constantly reborn, will be extinguished and satisfied in an eternal abundance. O beloved of my soul! Do I ever experience pleasure apart from you? Do I form any idea of happiness apart from you? You have been pleased to crown my days with satisfaction, in the midst of abundance and peace.

Do I not give preference to your favor and your love above all things?

Have I not placed in your favor and in your love all the pleasure and all the

<summary>This page introduces a prayer by the English author Elizabeth Rowe, focusing on the soul's intense longing for God as the only source of true happiness and satisfaction.</summary>

consolation of my life? What use to me is this vain and deceitful picture of perishable things? My thoughts soar far beyond all these celestial spheres <note>original: "globes." In 18th-century cosmology and devotional literature, this refers to planets or the stars within the heavens.</note>. This earthly world holds no charms for me; I am dead to the empty and fleeting brilliance it puts on display. My soul is occupied with nothing but you; it incessantly sends up ardent and boundless longings <note>original: "voeux." With what eagerness my soul traverses these vast spaces of pleasure and happiness, of which you are the center!

How all other thoughts fade from my mind, except for yours! I forget myself, I forget everything, except you, the sublime object with which I am constantly occupied. She <note>Referencing "l'âme" (the soul), which is feminine in French.</note> will always be pre-
